# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but 
doc-11#chunk-001 · score=0.4199605255658081
-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple cause

# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improv

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.3123945605252806
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.2487341690815456
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.22965760608731278
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.
rag-007 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-direct · fake-chat

Answer

It maps text to a vector so semantic similarity can be computed.

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.21926450482675744
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea
doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.15386436372416595
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

Answer

It lets readers continue while one coordinator serializes writes.

# Reproducible Deployment

ID: doc-12

## Definition

A reproducible run records the commit SHA, dataset hash, prompt hash, configuration, model IDs, and random seed.
SQLite WAL mode lets readers continue while a coordinator serializes experiment writes.

## Trade-offs

Local SQLite keeps deployment simple, but it is not a substitute for a distributed high-write database.

## Example

The runner writes case results first, then generates static JSON and HTML reports from the committed experiment record.

## Limitations

Reproducibility metadata cannot compensate for a provider that silently cha

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.
rag-026 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-direct · fake-chat

Answer

Content hashes detect changed sources, and chunk overlap repeats boundary text.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.2374587360434655
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.16381273202031824
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.
rag-027 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-direct · fake-chat

Answer

Chunking creates bounded retrieval units, and cache keys bind their vectors to text, preprocessing, and model identity.

Citations: doc-03, doc-04

# RAG System Overview

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.2313259727864961
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead
rag-031 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-direct · fake-chat

Answer

Use a defined prompt variant, then report retrieval and generation metrics separately.

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.3086066999241839
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead
rag-003 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.3123945605252806
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.
rag-005 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

It divides normalized documents into retrieval units that fit model limits.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.2487341690815456
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.22965760608731278
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.
rag-007 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

It maps text to a vector so semantic similarity can be computed.

Citations: doc-04

Retrieved evidence

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.21926450482675744
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

ID: doc-12

## Example

The runner writes case results first, then generates static JSON and HTML reports from the committed experiment record.

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.
rag-026 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Content hashes detect changed sources, and chunk overlap repeats boundary text.

Citations: doc-02, doc-03

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.2374587360434655
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.16381273202031824
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.
rag-027 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Chunking creates bounded retrieval units, and cache keys bind their vectors to text, preprocessing, and model identity.

Citations: doc-03, doc-04

Retrieved evidence

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr
rag-028 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Embeddings represent text as vectors, and dense retrieval compares query and chunk vectors with cosine similarity.

Citations: doc-04, doc-05

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.5231388366621642
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-01#chunk-000 · score=0.36265926036768287
# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.32025630761017443
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

Answer

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.2313259727864961
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead
rag-031 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Use a defined prompt variant, then report retrieval and generation metrics separately.

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.32444284226152514
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.
rag-035 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Preserve whether evidence was retrieved and record commit, dataset, prompt, configuration, models, and seed.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.3086066999241839
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Reproducible Deployment

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.2211629342323457
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-09#chunk-000 · score=0.21459876881973805
# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.22880215766121476
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.3123945605252806
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.2487341690815456
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.22965760608731278
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.16754156331667822
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.295084445425327
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

doc-04#chunk-001 · score=0.15811388300841897
, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.1475422227126635
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
rag-017 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-direct · fake-chat

Answer

Explicitly declining when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.

Citations: doc-09

doc-09#chunk-000 · score=0.2621894101377849
# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea
doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.19738550848793074
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-01#chunk-000 · score=0.14098147537004832
# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.18650096164806276
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.3471050672503118
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-10#chunk-000 · score=0.29918303680270636
# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.2374587360434655
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-10#chunk-000 · score=0.48280454958526764
# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr
doc-01#chunk-000 · score=0.4307055216465324
# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o
doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.4271410714851652
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.4047619047619048
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
rag-032 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-direct · fake-chat

Answer

Separate retrieval from generation metrics and report false-answer rate on unanswerable cases.

doc-09#chunk-000 · score=0.24678382369818683
# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.17583479290554324
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.17180824278583567
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.
rag-033 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-direct · fake-chat

Answer

Measure false answers on unsupported cases and run a buffered cost preflight using verified prices.

Citations: doc-09, doc-10

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.32152064852223783
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.25131234497501725
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.26452002850644335
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.31201886037669146
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
doc-09#chunk-000 · score=0.3254070133091433
# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea
doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.3095238095238096
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.02512594538148031
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.3123945605252806
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.22965760608731278
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.16754156331667822
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea
doc-01#chunk-000 · score=0.29192017967990475
# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.31239456052528064
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.295084445425327
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.25819888974716115
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.
rag-013 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Use only supplied evidence and cite stable document IDs.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.6382847385042256
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.3706922916570217
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.21926450482675744
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.32904509826424905
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

Local SQLite keeps deployment simple, but it is not a substitute for a distributed high-write database.

## Example

The runner writes case results first, then generates static JSON and HTML reports from the committed experiment record.

## Limitations

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.2374587360434655
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.16381273202031824
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-11#chunk-001 · score=0.1556997888323046
s, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead of forcing certainty.

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.11826247919781659
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

Answer

Chunking creates bounded retrieval units, and cache keys bind their vectors to text, preprocessing, and model identity.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.2041241452319315
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.
rag-028 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · fake-chat

Answer

Embeddings represent text as vectors, and dense retrieval compares query and chunk vectors with cosine similarity.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.5231388366621642
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

ID: doc-01

## Definition

A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.4047619047619048
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.25131234497501725
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea
doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.21952851997938078
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.1458029608799511
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

# Reproducible Deployment

ID: doc-12

## Definition

A reproducible run records the commit SHA, dataset hash, prompt hash, configuration, model IDs, and random seed.
SQLite WAL mode lets readers continue while a coordinator serializes experiment writes.

## Trade-offs

Local SQLite keeps deployment simple, but it is not a substitute for a distributed high-write database.

## Example

The runner writes case results first, then generates static JSON and HTML reports from the committed experiment record.

## Limitations

Reproducibility metadata cannot compensate for a provider that silently cha

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.20801257358446104
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-11#chunk-001 · score=0.18257418583505536
s, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead of forcing certainty.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.12309149097933275
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.03750293003086747
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-01#chunk-000 · score=0.031083493608010458
# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.13135173437318345
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.12469593727770997
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr
